〔U1272 para〕
為了回應「《伊里亞德》卷十八寫於《奧德賽》之後，因而可能是它借用了《奧德賽》」這種可能的反對意見，我要把第 108 行連同上下文一併引出。這段文字（卷十八 107–110 行）是：──
> To meet the possible objection that *Il*. XVIII. was written later than the *Odyssey*, and might therefore have borrowed from it, I will quote the context of line 108 as well as the line itself. The passage runs (XVIII. 107-110):—

〔U1273 para〕
ὦς ἔρις ἔκ τε θεῶν ἔκ τ' ἀνθρώπων άπόλοιτο καὶ χόλος ὅς τ' ἐφέηκε πολύφρονά περ χαλεπῆναι, ὅς τε πολὺ γλυκίων μέλιτος καταλειβόμενοιο ἀνδρῶν ἐν στήθεσσιν ἀέξεται ἠύτε καπνός.（但願紛爭從神界和人間消失，還有那怒氣──它使人即使再明智也會暴躁起來；它比流淌的蜂蜜還要甜，在人們胸中像煙一樣滋長膨脹。）
> ὦς ἔρις ἔκ τε θεῶν ἔκ τ' ἀνθρώπων άπόλοιτο καὶ χόλος ὅς τ' ἐφέηκε πολύφρονά περ χαλεπῆναι, ὅς τε πολὺ γλυκίων μέλιτος καταλειβόμενοιο ἀνδρῶν ἐν στήθεσσιν ἀέξεται ἠύτε καπνός.

〔U1274 para〕
我認為從這段高貴的文字衍生出來的那行《奧德賽》詩句，其上下文如下（卷十四 462–465 行）：──
> The context of the Odyssean line which I suppose to be derived from this noble passage is as follows (xiv. 462-465):—

〔U1275 para〕
κέκλυθι νῦν Ἐύμαιε, καὶ ἄλλοι πάντες εταῖροι· εὐξάμενός τι ἔπος ἐρέω· οἶνος γὰρ ἀνώγει ἠλεός, ὅς τ' ἐφέηκε πολύφρονά περ μάλ' ἀεȋσαι καὶ θ' ἁπαλόν γελάσαι, καί τ' ὀρχήσαθαι ἀνῆκεν,（歐邁俄斯，還有其他所有的夥伴，現在聽我說；我要誇口說一句話，因為是酒叫我這麼做的──那令人昏亂的酒，它使人即使再明智也會放聲高歌、輕聲嬉笑，還引人起身跳舞，）
> κέκλυθι νῦν Ἐύμαιε, καὶ ἄλλοι πάντες εταῖροι· εὐξάμενός τι ἔπος ἐρέω· οἶνος γὰρ ἀνώγει ἠλεός, ὅς τ' ἐφέηκε πολύφρονά περ μάλ' ἀεȋσαι καὶ θ' ἁπαλόν γελάσαι, καί τ' ὀρχήσαθαι ἀνῆκεν,

〔U1276 para〕
哪一種比較可能──是《伊里亞德》那幾行壯麗的詩句由《奧德賽》卷十四 464 行發展而來，還是這一行是無意識地改編自《伊里亞德》卷十八 108 行？因為這兩行是父子關係，這一點幾乎不會有人爭辯。
> Which is the most likely—that the magnificent Iliadic lines were developed from *Od*. xiv. 464, or that this line is an unconscious adaptation from *Il*. XVIII. 108? For that the two lines are father and son will hardly be disputed.

〔U1277 para〕
再問，哪一種說法比較說得通──是《伊里亞德》卷十八的作者拿奧德修斯燒洗澡水的段落來替帕特羅克洛斯燒水，還是《奧德賽》的作者略去了關於帕特羅克洛斯的那一行，拿這段的其餘部分來替奧德修斯燒洗澡水？
> Which again commends itself best—that the writer of *Il*. XVIII. took the heating of Ulysses' bath water to heat water for Patroclus, or that the writer of the *Odyssey* omitted the line about Patroclus, and used the rest of the passage to heat water for Ulysses' bath?

〔U1278 para〕
至於向忒提斯致意的那兩段話（《伊里亞德》卷十八 385–387 行與 424–427 行），哪一種比較可能──是《伊里亞德》卷十八的作者把《奧德賽》卷五 87–91 行這顆櫻桃分成兩口吃，還是《奧德賽》的作者只需要一段致意，便把《伊里亞德》的兩段合而為一，如上文所引？
> As regards the two salutations to Thetis (*Il*. XVIII. 385-387, and 424-427), is it more likely that the writer of *Il*. XVIII. made two bites of the Odyssean cherry of v. 87-91, or that the writer of the *Odyssey*, wanting but a single salutation, combined the two Iliadic ones as in the passage above given?

〔U1279 para〕
最後，赫淮斯托斯放在阿基里斯盾牌上的那一串星座，比較可能是由《奧德賽》卷五 272–275 行擴充而來，還是後面這幾行是經過必要的修改、取自《伊里亞德》卷十八 486–489 行？不論《奧德賽》的年代為何，我都毫不懷疑《伊里亞德》卷十八的年代一定更早；然而我們傑出的荷馬學者，對《伊里亞德》其他任何一卷都不像對這一卷這樣滿腹小小的牢騷，也不像對這一卷這樣一致認定是竄入的增補。如果說《伊里亞德》有哪一部分最能顯出荷馬的無可企及，那就是他對阿基里斯之盾的描寫。
> Lastly, is the list of constellations which Vulcan put on to the shield of Achilles more likely to have been amplified from *Od*. v. 272-275, or these last-named lines to have been taken, with such modification as was necessary, from *Il*. XVIII. 486-489? Whatever may be the date of the *Odyssey*, I cannot doubt that *Il*. XVIII. must be dated earlier; and yet there is no Book of the *Iliad* about which our eminent Homeric scholars are more full of small complaints, or more unanimous in regarding as an interpolation. If there is one part of the *Iliad* rather than another in which Homer shows himself unapproachable, it is in his description of the shield of Achilles.

〔U1280 para〕
我要再向讀者保證，《伊里亞德》各卷被取用的自由程度，似乎都和我剛才詳細討論的那幾卷一樣；我也找不到《奧德賽》有哪一部分向《伊里亞德》借用得比全詩其餘部分少。偶爾題材不同，使作者連續三四頁都沒有一處《伊里亞德》的拼綴，但這很少見。每頁一兩處，甚至有時三四處《伊里亞德》的段落，比較接近平均數；不過其中有些屬於可稱為套語的東西。
> I will again assure the reader that all the Books of the *Iliad* seem drawn from with the same freedom as that shown in those which I have now dealt with in detail, and also that I can find no part of the *Odyssey* which borrows any less freely from the *Iliad* than the rest of the poem; here and there difference of subject leads the writer to go three or four pages without a single Iliadic cento, but this is rare. One or two, or even sometimes three or four, Iliadic passages in a page is nearer the average, but of these some will be what may be called common form.

〔U1281 para〕
它們出現得如此頻繁，卻不會讓人聯想到剽竊，正如《天路歷程》裡引用的《聖經》文字，即使刪去出處，也不會讓人聯想到剽竊一樣。它們深深嵌進上下文，是結構性的而非裝飾性的；這也排除了它們是抄寫者或編者添加進去的可能。它們似乎是作者對《伊里亞德》爛熟於胸、自然流露的產物。同樣明顯的是，她並不是在為別人的作品寫摘要；她是從她自己、她的家鄉、她的同胞，以及整個西西里島的角度，*從頭*講述這個故事。其他民族和地方或許還能容忍，但在她心中激不起任何熱情。
> Their frequency raises no suggestion of plagiarism any more than the Biblical quotations in *Pilgrim's Progress* would do if the references were cut out. They are so built into the context as to be structural, not ornamental; and to preclude the idea of their having been added by copyists or editors. They seem to be the spontaneous outcome of the fullness of the writer's knowledge of the *Iliad*. It is also evident that she is not making a resumé of other people's works; she is telling the story *de novo* from the point of view of herself, her home, her countrymen, and the whole island of Sicily. Other peoples and places may be tolerated, but they raise no enthusiasm in her mind.

〔U1282 para〕
儘管如此，《奧德賽》與史詩集成中的所有詩篇之間，必定存在某種風格與情感上的相似，而且從那些失傳詩篇至今殘存的斷片，確實可以證明這種相似存在過。在一切藝術中，不論是文學、繪畫、音樂還是建築，同一時代、同一國度總會有某種共同的特質。每個時代都有它慣用的藝術題材；原因在於，讀者、觀眾或聽眾熟悉故事的大綱，比較方便。書面文學在這方面比繪畫或雕塑自由，因為它能解釋，也更能讓讀者對即將到來的內容有所準備。至於雖已寫成文字、卻主要是要對著一群幾乎不識字的聽眾朗誦的文學，它存在的條件是必須立刻訴諸理解，因此只會處理聽者應當已經知道大概的內容。作者──不論是他還是她──可以從國族慣用題材中任取一段，並在合理範圍內隨心所欲地處理，但必須和古老而熟悉的故事掛得上鉤；同一時代、同一語言、同一民族的同類詩篇之間某種風格上的相似，便由此而生。這一點對中世紀義大利畫家和對史詩集成同樣適用。它們呈現給我們的，是相異中的相似，也是相似中的相異。
> Nevertheless, a certain similarity of style and feeling between the *Odyssey* and all the poems of the Epic cycle is certain to have existed, and indeed can be proved to have existed from the fragments of the lost poems that still remain. In all art, whether literary, pictorial, musical, or architectural, a certain character will be common to a certain age and country. Every age has its stock subjects for artistic treatment; the reason for this is that it is convenient for the reader, spectator, or listener, to be familiar with the main outlines of the story. Written literature is freer in this respect than painting or sculpture, for it can explain and prepare the reader better for what is coming. Literature which, though written, is intended mainly for recitation before an audience few of whom can read, exists only on condition of its appealing instantly to the understanding, and will, therefore, deal only with what the hearer is supposed already to know in outline. The writer may take any part of the stock national subjects that he or she likes, and within reasonable limits may treat it according to his or her fancy, but it must hitch on to the old familiar story, and hence will arise a certain similarity of style between all poems of the same class that belong to the same age, language, and people. This holds just as good for the medieval Italian painters as it does for the Epic cycle. They offer us a similarity in dissimilarity and a dissimilarity in similarity.

〔U1283 para〕
然而，我們若記得，《奧德賽》的風格不但勢必向當時現存的所有其他史詩靠攏，而且作者的心靈──且不說其他史詩集成詩人──還深深浸透了荷馬的心靈，程度正如我們所見；那麼，這樣加在一個女人作品上的一層陽剛的飾面，竟遮掩了那較不顯眼、卻遠為確鑿的證據──證明作者和她所借用的那個男人或那些男人並不同性別──也就不足為奇了。
> When we remember, however, that the style of the *Odyssey* must not only perforce gravitate towards that of all the other then existing epic poems, but also that the writer's mind is as strongly leavened with the mind of Homer, let alone the other Cyclic poets, as we have seen it to be, it is not surprising that the veneer of virility thus given to a woman's work should have concealed the less patent, but far more conclusive, evidence that the writer was not of the same sex as the man, or men, from whom she was borrowing.

〔U1284 para〕
同時，儘管她利用荷馬，我認為她是在生他的氣，或許還有點嫉妒；關於這一點，我會在下一章多談。也許是他嘲笑女人、捉弄女人的方式激怒了她──他這麼做不是因為不喜歡她們，而是因為喜歡逗弄她們；她卻無意在這麼嚴肅的題目上開玩笑。我們已經看到，荷馬把一只三腳鼎的價值定為一個能幹耐用、什麼雜活都做的女人的三倍，她是怎樣反唇相譏的。再者，他對眾神全然的輕蔑──他毫不費心掩飾──對一位從不允許自己逾越牧師女兒偶爾那點溫和不敬的作者來說，想必很刺眼。因此，在我看來，她對待荷馬不無幾分嚴苛；這是我對她唯一要認真挑剔的毛病。
> At the same time, in spite of the use she makes of Homer, I think she was angry with him, and perhaps jealous; on which head I will say more in my next Chapter. Possibly the way he laughs at women and teases them, not because he dislikes them, but because he enjoys playing with them, irritates her; she was not disposed to play on such a serious subject. We have seen how she retorts on him for having made a tripod worth three times as much as a good serviceable woman of all work. His utter contempt, again, for the gods, which he is at no pains to conceal, would be offensive to a writer who never permits herself to go beyond the occasional mild irreverence of the Vicar's daughter. Therefore, she treats Homer, as its seems to me, not without a certain hardness; and this is the only serious fault I have to find with her.

〔U1285 para〕
例如，赫克托耳向安德洛瑪刻告別的結尾幾行，人們本以為她會不忍把這段文字挪作尋常之用，她卻拿來放進忒勒馬科斯口中，而他不過是叫母親回房去罷了。她在卷一 356–359 行這樣做，在卷二十一 350–353 行又這樣做。這可不應該。她拿那些為了洗淨可憐的帕特羅克洛斯屍身血污而燒的水（《伊里亞德》卷十八 344 行以下），用來給奧德修斯洗澡（《奧德賽》卷八 434–437 行），也同樣不應該。這裡的不敬，無疑比荷馬對眾神的任何不敬都要深。
> For example, she takes the concluding lines of Hector's farewell to Andromache, a passage which one would have thought she would have shrunk from turning to common uses, and puts it into the mouth of Telemachus when he is simply telling his mother to take herself off. She does this in i. 356-359 and again in xxi. 350-353. This is not as it should be. Nor yet again is her taking the water that was heated to wash the blood from the body of poor Patroclus (*Il*. XVIII. 344 &c.) and using it for Ulysses' bath (*Od*. viii. 434-437). Surely the disrespect here is deeper than any that can be found in Homer towards the gods.

〔U1286 para〕
不過，不論《奧德賽》的作者是以什麼樣的心態對待《伊里亞德》，我都毫不懷疑，她對這部詩以我們今天所見的形態熟悉得很；而這一點，正是我認為值得在上面這一章花這麼多篇幅來力求證實的。
> But, whatever the spirit may have been in which the writer of the *Odyssey* has treated the *Iliad*, I cannot doubt that that she knew this poem exceedingly well in the shape in which we have it, and this is the point which I have thought it worth while to endeavour to substantiate at such length in the foregoing Chapter.

〔U1287 footnote〕
ἡυεῖς δέ κλέος οἷον ἀκούομεν, οὐδέ τι ἴδμεν,（我們只聽到傳聞，什麼也不知道，）《伊里亞德》卷二 486 行。
> ἡυεῖς δέ κλέος οἷον ἀκούομεν, οὐδέ τι ἴδμεν, *Il*. II. 486.

〔U1288 heading〕
第十五章
> CHAPTER XV.

〔U1289 para〕
《奧德賽》與特洛伊史詩集成其他詩篇的關係，以及它在女作者手中的發展
> THE ODYSSEY IN ITS RELATION TO THE OTHER POEMS OF THE TROJAN CYCLE, AND ITS DEVELOPMENT IN THE HANDS OF THE AUTHORESS.

〔U1290 para〕
《奧德賽》的作者似乎知道史詩集成裡大部分已經失傳、與特洛伊相關的詩篇──這類詩共有八部；不過，我們對這些詩的全部認識，只來自普羅克洛斯殘篇裡的提要，以及後世作者零星引用的幾行詩句，所以即使她提到的事件，普羅克洛斯明說是某一部詩所講述的，我們也無法確鑿無疑地斷定她手邊就有那部詩。儘管如此，從《奧德賽》卷一與卷三的若干段落看來，她很可能知道《歸返記》，也就是亞該亞人自特洛伊歸返的故事；我們也可以推想，涅斯托爾那幾篇長篇大論（《奧德賽》卷三 102–200 行與 253–328 行）主要就取材於此，因為其中有些細節，和普羅克洛斯所作的《歸返記》提要若合符節。
> The writer of the *Odyssey* appears to have known most of those lost poems of the Epic cycle—eight in number—that relate to Troy, but as all we know about them is from the summaries given in the fragment of Proclus, and from a few lines here and there quoted in later authors, we can have no irrefragable certainty that she had the poems before her even when she alludes to incidents mentioned by Proclus as being dealt with in any given one of them. Nevertheless, passages in *Od*. i. and iii. make it probable that she knew the *Nosti* or the Return of the Achæans from Troy, and we may suppose that Nestor's long speeches (*Od*. iii. 102-200 and 253-328) are derived mainly from this source, for they contain particulars that correspond closely with the epitome of the *Nosti* given by Proclus.

〔U1291 para〕
這樣我們就能解釋，為什麼涅斯托爾的話裡對愛琴海的地形描述得如此正確，而詩中其他地方，除了光知道提洛島上有阿波羅的神殿（《奧德賽》卷六 162 行），以及偶爾提一下克里特之外，就再也看不出這種知識。我看到傑布教授說，《奧德賽》「對提洛島顯出熟悉的認識」；[註1]可是詩中沒有任何一處足以支持這個斷言。
> We can thus explain the correctness of the topography of the Ægæan sea that is manifested in Nestor's speeches, but no where else in the poem beyond a bare knowledge of the existence of Apollo's shrine in Delos (*Od*. vi. 162) and an occasional mention of Crete. I see Professor Jebb says that the *Odyssey* "shows a familiar knowledge of Delos;"[註1] but there is no warrant for this assertion from anything in the poem.

〔U1292 para〕
《奧德賽》的作者在卷四裡，似乎也知道《庫普里亞》；這部詩講的是導致特洛伊戰爭的種種事件。
> The writer of the *Odyssey* seems, in Book iv., to have also known the *Cypria*, which dealt with the events that led up to the Trojan war.

〔U1293 para〕
《奧德賽》卷二十四（35–97 行）暗示作者知道《埃提俄匹斯》。提到門農的那一處（《奧德賽》卷十一 522 行）也是如此。
> Book xxiv. of the *Odyssey* (35-97) suggests a knowledge of the *Æthiopis*. So also does the mention of Memnon (*Od*. xi. 522).

〔U1294 para〕
作者可能知道《小伊里亞德》，這可以從《奧德賽》卷四 271–283 行看出端倪──那裡的海倫似乎已改嫁得伊福玻斯──也可以從卷十一 543–562 行看出來；此外還有卷十一 508、509 行，奧德修斯在那裡說是他把涅俄普托勒摩斯帶到斯庫羅斯去的。奧德修斯扮作探子潛入特洛伊（《奧德賽》卷四 242–256 行），普羅克洛斯也列為《小伊里亞德》的情節之一。因此，我看不出《奧德賽》作者熟悉《小伊里亞德》這件事還有什麼可懷疑的；這部詩篇幅顯然不長，只有四卷。
> Knowledge of the *Little Iliad* may be suspected from *Od*. iv. 271-283, where Helen seems to be now married to Deiphobus, and from xi. 543-562; as also from xi. 508, 509, where Ulysses says that he took Neoptolemus to Scyrus. Ulysses entering Troy as a spy (*Od*. iv. 242-256) is also given by Proclus as one of the incidents in the *Little Iliad*. I do not see, therefore, that there can be much doubt about the writer of the *Odyssey* having been acquainted with the *Little Iliad*, a poem which was apparently of no great length, being only in four Books.

〔U1295 para〕
特洛伊人圍著木馬開會議論的那一段（《奧德賽》卷八 492–517 行），則出自兩卷本的《伊利昂陷落記》。
> From the two Books of the *Sack of Troy* we get the account of the council held by the Trojans over the wooden horse (*Od*. viii. 492-517).

〔U1296 para〕
我們已經看到，《奧德賽》的女作者對《伊里亞德》有多熟悉；那麼八部特洛伊詩篇當中，她似乎不知道的只剩一部──我指的是《忒勒戈尼亞》。一般都把這部詩的年代定在《奧德賽》之後，而且照理說這也是對的；但即使它比《奧德賽》早，我們也可以確定《奧德賽》的作者會對它視而不見，因為這部詩幾乎不可能支持她替潘妮洛碧塑造的那種品格。
> We have seen how familiar the authoress of the *Odyssey* was with the *Iliad*; there only remains, therefore, one of the eight Trojan poems which she does not appear to have known—I mean the *Telegony*, which is generally, and one would say correctly, placed later than the *Odyssey*; but even though it were earlier we may be sure that the writer of the *Odyssey* would have ignored it, for it will hardly bear her out in the character she has given of Penelope.

〔U1297 para〕
順帶一提：荷馬（當然是指《伊里亞德》的作者）雖然偶爾說出一些令人聯想到《庫普里亞》的話，但對普羅克洛斯列為特洛伊史詩集成其他各部主題的那些事件，他卻沒有一行詩哪怕只是暗示知道其中任何一件；因此推論起來，除了《庫普里亞》或許──而且很不確定──以外，那些詩在他寫作之前似乎一部也還沒有問世。不過，我們也不能確定事情就是如此。
> In passing I may say that though Homer (meaning, of course, the writer of the *Iliad*) occasionally says things that suggest the Cypria, there is not a line that even suggests knowledge of a single one of the incidents given by Proclus as forming the subjects of the other Books of the Trojan cycle; the inference, therefore, would seem to be that none of them, except possibly, though very uncertainly, the Cypria, had appeared before he wrote. Nevertheless we cannot be sure that this was so.

〔U1298 para〕
這就引出一個奇特的問題：《奧德賽》的作者一方面大量取用《伊里亞德》，另一方面對普羅克洛斯所列、特洛伊史詩集成其他詩篇講過的幾乎所有事件，也毫不避諱地或詳或略加以處理，那她為什麼偏偏避開《伊里亞德》裡的每一件事，一件也不提？
> The curious question now arises why the writer of the *Odyssey* should have avoided referring to a single Iliadic incident, while showing no unwillingness to treat more or less fully of almost all those mentioned by Proclus as dealt with in the other poems of the Trojan cycle, and also while laying the *Iliad* under such frequent contributions.

〔U1299 para〕
我記得曾經對一位大出版商說，某本書顯然大大受惠於另一本書，卻對那本書隻字未提。那位出版商問：「這位作者有沒有提到其他講同一題目的現代著作？」我回答：「當然有。」他說：「那我告訴你，我們幾乎一成不變的經驗是：一個作者列舉了好些別的書，卻漏掉一本他明明借用過的，那麼被漏掉的那本，正是給他啟發最多的那一本。」他這番話似乎解開了我的困惑：為什麼《奧德賽》的作者對《伊里亞德》的情節如此敬而遠之。那正是她想要與之匹敵──甚至想要取而代之──的詩。她知道那是特洛伊史詩集成中最出色的一部，而且熟到書裡合用的詩句不斷自己湧上心頭──合用的詩句若不拿來挪用，又有什麼用處？她知道自己應付其他詩篇綽綽有餘，對《伊里亞德》卻沒有那麼篤定，所以凡是《伊里亞德》已經佔據的地盤，她一寸也不肯踏進去。
> I remember saying to a great publisher that a certain book was obviously much indebted to a certain other book to which no reference was made. "Has the writer," said the publisher in question, "referred to other modern books on the same subject?" I answered, "Certainly." "Then," said he, "let me tell you that it is our almost unvaried experience that when a writer mentions a number of other books, and omits one which he has evidently borrowed from, the omitted book is the one which has most largely suggested his own." His words seemed to explain my difficulty about the way in which the writer of the *Odyssey* lets the incidents of the *Iliad* so severely alone. It was the poem she was trying to rival, if not to supersede. She knew it to be far the finest of the Trojan cycle; she was so familiar with it that appropriate lines from it were continually suggesting themselves to her—and what is an appropriate line good for if it is not to be appropriated? She knew she could hold her own against the other poems, but she did not feel so sure about the *Iliad*, and she would not cover any of the ground which it had already occupied.

〔U1300 para〕
當然，總還有另一種可能的解釋：《奧德賽》的作者也許聽過一些有關荷馬私生活的傳聞，心裡很不以為然。他也許打過老婆，或者拐走了別人的老婆，或者兩樣都幹過，又或者做了上百件別的事，讓他不完全是阿瑞忒會樂意讓女兒多加理睬的那種人──除非萬不得已。不過我相信，上一段的解釋最合情理。
> Of course there is always this other explanation possible, I mean that traditions about Homer's private life may have been known to the writer of the *Odyssey*, which displeased her. He may have beaten his wife, or run away with somebody else's, or both, or done a hundred things which made him not exactly the kind of person whom Arete would like her daughter to countenance more than was absolutely necessary. I believe, however, that the explanation given in the preceding paragraph is the most reasonable.

〔U1301 para〕
現在讓我說明，在我看來《奧德賽》在這位女詩人手中是怎麼發展起來的。我不認為她一開始就存心要寫一部篇幅浩大的史詩。這部作品似乎是從小小的開頭在她手中一點一點長出來的，每多寫一次，就替更進一步的發展開了一扇門，直到最後《奧德賽》就這麼成形了──與其說是精心觀察經營出來的東西，不如說是自然而然長成的。假如它是經營出來的，無疑會少掉許多反常、矛盾、荒謬和小小的疏漏──任何一部事先沒有把通盤計畫想清楚的長篇作品，在發展過程中都免不了這些毛病。可是，少了這些，它肯定也會失去不少魅力。
> And now let me explain what I consider to have been the development of the *Odyssey* in the hands of the poetess. I cannot think that she deliberately set herself to write an epic poem of great length. The work appears to have grown on her hands piecemeal from small beginnings, each additional effort opening the door for further development, till at last there the *Odyssey* was—a spontaneous growth rather than a thing done by observation. Had it come by observation, no doubt it would have been freer from the anomalies, inconsistencies, absurdities, and small slovenlinesses which are inseparable from the development of any long work, the plan of which has not been fully thought out beforehand. But surely in losing these it would have lost not a little of its charm.

〔U1302 para〕
從傑布教授的《荷馬導論》（1888 年版，第 131 頁）我看到，他同意基希霍夫的看法，認為《奧德賽》包含「來自不同來源與不同時期、層次分明的詩歌材料」，又認為這首詩今天形式上的統一應歸功於一個人；他接著說：──
> From Professor Jebb's *Introduction to Homer*, Ed. 1888, p. 131, I see that he agrees with Kirchhoff in holding that the *Odyssey* contains "distinct strata of poetical material from different sources and periods," and also that the poem owes its present unity of form to one man; he continues:—

〔U1303 quote〕
但在這種形式的統一之下，仍可察覺到一個過程的痕跡：不同的作品曾經被拿來彼此調適。
> But under this unity of form there are perceptible traces of a process by which different compositions were adapted to one another.

〔U1304 para〕
在前一頁的一條註裡，他告訴我們，基希霍夫認為卷一最前面的 87 行，原是原始的《奧德修斯歸鄉》的開篇引子。
> In a note on the preceding page he tells us that Kirchhoff regards the first 87 verses of Book i. as having formed the exordium of the original Return of Ulysses.

〔U1305 para〕
我自己的結論──就我所記得，是在讀到傑布教授《荷馬導論》的任何一個字之前得出的──和上述看法大部分相合。我發現《奧德賽》是由兩首各自獨立、目標迥然不同的詩合成一部作品；合得並非不巧妙，但還沒有巧妙到能掩飾計畫改變的地步。這兩首詩是：一、奧德修斯造訪費埃克斯人，以及他親口講述的歷險故事。二、潘妮洛碧與求婚者的故事，附帶忒勒馬科斯航往皮洛斯的插曲。兩者之中，第一首寫成的時候，作者還根本沒有打算處理第二首；而到了最後，第二首卻變得比第一首更重要。
> My own conclusions, arrived at to the best of my belief before I had read a word of Professor Jebb's *Introduction*, agree in great part with the foregoing. I found the *Odyssey* to consist of two distinct poems, with widely different aims, and united into a single work, not unskilfully, but still not so skilfully as to conceal a change of scheme. The two poems are: 1. The visit of Ulysses to the Phæacians, with the story of his adventures as related by himself. 2. The story of Penelope and the suitors, with the episode of Telemachus's voyage to Pylos. Of these two, the first was written before the writer had any intention of dealing with the second, while the second in the end became more important than the first.

> Tell me, oh Muse, of that ingenious hero who travelled far and wide after he had sacked the strong citadel of Troy. He saw many cities and learned the manners of many nations; moreover, he suffered much by sea while trying to save his own life and bring his men safely home; but do what he might he could not save his men, for they perished through their own sheer folly in eating the cattle of the Sun-god Hyperion; so the god prevented them from ever getting home. Tell me too about all these things, oh daughter of Jove, from whatever source you may know them (i. 1-10).

I had thought that the point of juncture between the two poems was in the middle of line 187, and that the ἔγρετο in the second half of the line had perhaps been originally εὖδεν; but it must be somewhere close about this line, and I am quite ready to adopt Kirchhoff's opinion now that I have come to see why Ulysses was made to sleep so profoundly on leaving Scheria.

〔U1314 para〕
現在我並不認為，關於奧德修斯在伊薩卡醒來之後的遭遇，曾經有過現存版本以外的任何記述；我倒認為，作者正是在她必須把舊計畫收尾的那一點上，被引導去採用了新的計畫。因為到了這一點，她會頭一次正面碰上這個難題：把奧德修斯送到伊薩卡之後，該拿他怎麼辦。
> I do not now think that there was ever any account of what happened to Ulysses on his waking up in Ithaca, other than what we now have, but rather that the writer was led to adopt a new scheme at the very point where it became incumbent upon her to complete an old one. For at this point she would first find herself face to face with the difficulty of knowing what to do with Ulysses in Ithaca after she had got him there.

〔U1315 para〕
她不能對求婚者完全置之不理；他們的存在和潘妮洛碧的放蕩實在太出名了。她不能讓奧德修斯和潘妮洛碧在求婚者還待在他家裡的時候歡喜團圓；而即使他把求婚者都殺了，他也絕不可能寬恕潘妮洛碧的行徑──身為史詩英雄，就不可能。《奧德賽》的作者顯然原以為自己能找到某種辦法擺脫這個困境，可是事到臨頭，她才發現自己只有兩條路：要嘛讓奧德修斯把妻子和求婚者一起殺掉，要嘛堅稱她自始至終都像新落的雪一樣純潔。她選了第二條路──她當然會這麼選──然後盡她所能，在聽眾面前厚著臉皮硬撐到底。因此，就在卷十三 187 行前後，她拔掉了「歸鄉」的營寨，另開一場新的戰役。
> She could not ignore the suitors altogether; their existence and Penelope's profligacy were too notorious. She could not make Ulysses and Penelope meet happily while the suitors were still in his house; and even though he killed them, he could never condone Penelope's conduct—not as an epic hero. The writer of the *Odyssey* had evidently thought that she could find some way out of the difficulty, but when it came to the point she discovered that she must either make Ulysses kill his wife along with the suitors, or contend that from first to last she had been pure as new fallen snow. She chose the second alternative, as she would be sure to do, and brazened it out with her audience as best she could. At line 187, therefore, of Book xiii. or thereabouts, she broke up her 'Return' camp and started on a new campaign.

〔U1316 para〕
為了把兩首詩連起來，她加進了卷十一 115–137 行，讓忒瑞西阿斯在那裡告訴奧德修斯求婚者的事，以及他殺了他們之後還得再去流浪的事。我推想，忒瑞西阿斯的預言原本結束的地方，和喀耳刻的預言結束的地方相同；喀耳刻把忒瑞西阿斯關於太陽神赫利俄斯的牛群的警告一字不改地重複一遍（卷十二 137–140 行），以這一行作結：
> To bring the two poems together she added lines xi. 115-137, in which Teiresias tells Ulysses about the suitors and his further wanderings when he shall have killed them. I suppose Teiresias' prophecy to have originally ended where Circe's does when she repeats his warning about the cattle of the Sun-god verbatim (xii. 137-140) with the line

〔U1317 para〕
ὀψὲ κακῶς νεῖαι ὸλέσας ἄπο πάντας ἑταίρους·（你將失去所有同伴，歷盡艱辛，很晚才回到家。）
> ὀψὲ κακῶς νεῖαι ὸλέσας ἄπο πάντας ἑταίρους·

〔U1318 para〕
加在忒瑞西阿斯原始預言上的那段文字，第一行（卷十一 115 行）也見於卷九 535 行，只略有出入；但它只說奧德修斯將會發現家中有麻煩，並沒有說是什麼麻煩。
> The first line of the addition to Teiresias' original prophecy (xi. 115) is also found with a slight variant in ix. 535, but it merely states that Ulysses will find trouble in his house, without mentioning what the trouble is to be.

〔U1319 para〕
除了上面提到的兩個例外，原始的詩（也就是卷一 1–79 行與卷五 28 行至卷十三 187 行前後）不僅沒有任何地方表示有意處理求婚者，而且還有一些省略，清楚顯示根本沒有這個意圖。在這首詩裡，詩人只請繆思歌唱奧德修斯的歸鄉。宙斯在眾神會議上講話時（卷一 32–43 行），心裡並沒有想到求婚者；假如作者當時已經打算把他們寫進來，他一定會想到的。在眾神一再的談話裡，尤其在卷五──也就是原始那首詩的卷一（見 36–42 行、288、289 行與 345 行）[註2]──看來奧德修斯最大的苦難，到他抵達斯刻里亞時就該結束了。再者，卡呂普索（卷五 206–208 行）想勸阻他離開，說他根本不知道自己回到家之前還得經歷多少磨難，可是她並沒有再加一句，說他到了伊薩卡之後，最糟的還在後頭，來加強自己的說法。至於奧德修斯的母親在冥府裡的沉默，我已經討論過了。
> With the two exceptions above noted, there is not only nothing in the original poem (*i.e*., Book i. 1-79 and v. 28—xiii. 187 or thereabouts) to indicate any intention of dealing with the suitors, but there are omissions which make it plain that no such intention existed. In the poem the Muse is only asked to sing the Return of Ulysses. In the speech of Jove at the council of the gods (i. 32-43), he is not thinking about the suitors, as he would assuredly do if the writer had as yet meant to introduce them. In repeated speeches of the gods, and especially in Book v. which is Book i. of the original poem (see lines 36-42, 288, 289, and 345),[註2] it seems that Ulysses' most serious troubles were to end when he had reached Scheria. So again Calypso (v. 206-208) tries to deter him from leaving her by saying that he little knows what he will have to go through before he gets home again, but she does not enforce her argument by adding that when he had got to Ithaca the worst was yet to come. I have already dealt with the silence of Ulysses' mother in Hades.

〔U1320 para〕
因此，有鑒於省略比詩行更能透露計畫──因為把新題材嫁接到舊題材上時，為了使作品統一，必要的地方一定會插進詩行──我毫不猶豫地相信，卷一 1–79 行與卷五 28 行至卷十三 187 行前後，就是女作者為原始那首詩所寫的全部；我之所以更不猶豫地採納這個結論，是因為我雖然相信自己是獨立得出它的──任何人只要用應有的專注研讀《奧德賽》，都必然如此──卻發現自己儘管在細節上和基希霍夫多有出入，大體上仍與他一致；只是我無法承認這兩首詩出自兩個或更多不同的人之手。
> Noting, therefore, that omission is a more telling indication of scheme than lines which, when a new subject is being grafted on to an old one, are certain to be inserted where necessary in order to unify the work, I have no hesitation in believing that Books i. 1-79 and v. 28—xiii. 187 or thereabouts, formed as much as the authoress ever wrote of the original poem; I have the less hesitation in adopting this conclusion because, though I believe that I came to it independently as any one must do who studies the *Odyssey* with due attention, I find myself in substantial agreement with Kirchhoff in spite of much difference of detail, for I cannot admit that the two poems are by two or more separate people.

〔U1321 para〕
加進卷十一 115–137 行與卷九 535 行，再在卷五開頭新寫一場眾神會議，以取代被移到卷一 1–79 行的那一場──為了讓舊計畫和新計畫哪怕看起來有一點統一，為了掩飾繆思明明被請來歌唱一個題目，卻花了三分之二的時間去唱一個截然不同的題目，還唱到一個誰也沒請她唱的高潮，作者所做的就只有這幾件事。因為粗略地說，「歸鄉」佔了八卷，潘妮洛碧與求婚者佔了十六卷。
> The introduction of lines xi. 115-137 and of line ix. 535, with a writing of a new Council of the gods at the beginning of Book v. to take the place of the one that was removed to Book i. 1-79, were the only things that were done to give even a semblance of unity to the old scheme and the new, and to conceal the fact that the Muse after being asked to sing of one subject spends two thirds of her time in singing a very different one, with a climax for which no one had asked her. For, roughly, the Return occupies eight Books and Penelope and the suitors sixteen.

〔U1322 para〕
卷十一 115–137 行在寫卷十三的時候還不存在，這一點可以由下面這件事證明：奧德修斯對費埃克斯人說，他希望回家時看到妻子和親友一起平安度日（卷十三 42、43 行）。假如忒瑞西阿斯早已告訴他，他家裡會擠滿正在吞吃他家產的仇敵，而他非得把他們殺掉不可，他就不可能說出這種話。他已經發現忒瑞西阿斯對太陽神赫利俄斯的牛群的預言完全應驗，就幾乎不可能把這樣一個預言忘掉。事實上，他還把自己造訪冥府、會見忒瑞西阿斯的事告訴了潘妮洛碧（卷二十三 323 行），可見他是記得的。同樣明顯的是（從卷十三 382 行起），奧德修斯那時是頭一次從雅典娜那裡得知求婚者的事──假如忒瑞西阿斯的預言早已寫好，就不可能如此。
> That lines xi. 115-137 were non-existent when Book xiii. was being written is demonstrated by the fact of Ulysses' saying to the Phæacians that he hoped he should find his wife living with her friends in peace (xiii. 42, 43). He could not have said this if Teiresias had already told him that his house would be full of enemies who were eating up his estate, and whom he would have to kill. He could hardly forget such a prophecy after having found Teiresias quite correct about the cattle of the Sun-god. Indeed he tells Penelope about his visit to Hades and his interview with Teiresias (xxiii. 323), so it is plain he remembered it. It is plain, again (from xiii. 382 &c.), that Ulysses was then learning from Minerva about the suitors for the first time—which could not be if Teiresias' prophecy had been already written.

〔U1323 para〕
說來令人驚訝：明明只要再稍加修改，一切就能完全理順，作者卻甘心在這裡那裡留下一些段落，而她一定知道這些段落會洩露她作品的不夠一致；但我們應該非常感謝她，沒有更仔細地把它收拾整齊。比起她把作品弄得處處四平八穩，我們如今能從中認識她的地方要多得多，而我們珍視的是她本人，甚至勝過她的詩。她顯然寧可修補也不願刪除，這也難怪；因為如果真如極可能的情形那樣，這部作品是用硬化青銅、甚至鋼[註3]製成的尖筆刻在鉛板上的，修改起來就不會像我們今天這麼容易。何況我們大家只要做得到，也都寧可修修補補，而不願整段刪掉。卷四 621–624 行那段插入之所以顯得突兀，很可能──不過我幾乎不必說，這充其量只是一種可能──只是因為插入四行，不必把手稿割得太厲害，而較長的一段就得對手稿動更大的手術。
> It is surprising; seeing what a little further modification would have put everything quite straight, that the writer should have been content to leave passages here and there which she must have known would betray the want of homogeneity in her work, but we should be very thankful to her for not having tidied it up with greater care. We learn far more about her than we should do if she had made her work go more perfectly upon all fours, and it is herself that we value even more than her poem. She evidently preferred cobbling to cancelling, and small wonder, for if, as was very probably the case, the work was traced with a sharply pointed style of hardened bronze, or even steel,[註3] on plates of lead, alteration would not be so easy as it is with us. Besides, we all cobble rather than cancel if we can. It is quite possible, but I need hardly say that it is not more than a mere possibility, that the abruptness of the interpolation in Book iv. lines 621-624, may be due simply to its having been possible to introduce four lines without cutting the MS. about very badly, when a longer passage would have necessitated a more radical interference with it.

〔U1324 para〕
那麼，我們要在卷一 1–79 行與卷五 28 行至卷十三 187 行前後，或者更粗略地說，在卷五至卷十二（含）之中，去尋找這首詩的起點。這幾卷除了上面提到、加在忒瑞西阿斯預言上的那二十行之外，彼此並無矛盾；但它們的範圍並不一致，儘管在風格與處理手法上是一致的。它們自然分成兩組，每組四卷，也就是卷五至卷八與卷九至卷十二。第一組的寫作目的，是把奧德修斯帶到斯刻里亞，並展示費埃克斯人與作者本人──奧德修斯引起的興趣倒在其次；第二組的寫作目的，則是描述一趟環西西里的航行。
> We look, then, for the inception of the poem in Books i. 1-79 and v. 28-xiii. 187 or thereabouts, or more roughly in Books v.-xii. inclusive. These Books, though they contain no discrepancies among themselves except the twenty lines added to the prophecy of Teiresias above referred to are not homogeneous in scope, though they are so in style and treatment. They split themselves into two groups of four, *i.e*., v.-viii. and ix.-xii. The first group is written to bring Ulysses to Scheria and to exhibit the Phæacians and the writer herself—the interest in Ulysses being subordinate; the second is written to describe a periplus of Sicily.

〔U1325 para〕
卷九至卷十二似乎寫在卷五至卷八之前。這一點可以從雅典娜完全缺席推知。作者既然在卷五至卷八裡那樣隨意地讓女神出場，若說她會在故事裡有大好機會讓女神插手的時候，卻任由女神消失不見，那是不可想像的。這幾卷肯定與全詩其餘部分出自同一人之手。它們顯示出同等程度的《伊里亞德》影響；沒有哪個地方比這裡更明白地顯出女性的手筆；也沒有哪個地方比這裡更處處可見西西里，尤其是特拉帕尼的影子，不論是直接的還是間接的。我們之所以確信愛奧尼亞群島是照著埃加迪群島描畫的，正是從卷九開頭得來的；而奧德修斯的航行，如我已經說明過的，實際上是從埃律克斯山開始，到特拉帕尼結束。因此，我們大可完全拋開卷九至卷十二出自另一位作者的想法。
> Books ix.-xii. appear to have been written before Books v.-viii. We may gather this from the total absence of Minerva. It is inconceivable that having introduced the Goddess so freely in Books v.-viii. the writer should allow her to drop out from the story when there was such abundant scope for her interference. These Books are certainly by the same hand as the rest of the poem. They show the same amount of Iliadic influence; nowhere does a woman's hand appear more plainly; nowhere is Sicily, and more particularly Trapani, more in evidence, direct or indirect. It is from the beginning of Book ix. that we get our conviction that the Ionian islands were drawn from the Ægadean, and the voyages of Ulysses, as I have already shown, begin effectively with Mt. Eryx and end with Trapani. We may, therefore, dismiss all idea that Books ix.-xii. are by another writer.

〔U1326 para〕
雅典娜的缺席之所以無法解釋，除非假定在寫這幾卷的時候，作者還根本沒打算把她寫成後來那樣的 *dea ex machinâ*（機關降神的女神）；不僅如此，作者還顯出自己意識到女神在卷九至卷十二的缺席需要交代一下，於是讓奧德修斯責怪她，說他從離開特洛伊起就一直被她冷落，直到她帶他進了費埃克斯人的城市為止（卷十三 314–323 行）。女神替自己辯解說，她一直都知道他會平安回到家，而她之所以避開，是因為不想和她的叔父波賽頓爭吵──這個藉口我們在卷六末尾也看得到，然而就在那一卷裡，她卻一直在替奧德修斯增添風采，還在其他方面處處幫他的忙。我想，只要姪女不讓波賽頓看見，她再怎麼幫奧德修斯，他都不在乎吧。
> Not only is the absence of Minerva inexplicable except by supposing that at the time these Books were written it was no part of the writer's scheme to make her such a *dea ex machinâ* as she becomes later, but the writer shows herself aware that the absence of the goddess in Books ix.—xii. requires apology, and makes Ulysses upbraid her for having neglected him from the time he left Troy till she took him into the city of the Phæacians (xiii. 314-323). The goddess excuses herself by saying she had known all the time that he would get home quite safely, and had kept away because she did not want to quarrel with her uncle Neptune—an excuse which we also find at the end of Book vi., in which Book she has, nevertheless, been beautifying Ulysses and making herself otherwise useful to him. I suppose Neptune did not mind how much his niece helped Ulysses, provided she did not let him see her.

〔U1327 para〕
我知道我自己的書，尤其是早期那幾本，是怎樣被刪來改去、重新編排、更動計畫，又東補西綴來掩飾改動的痕跡，以至於我從來都要等到書寫完四分之三，才真正弄清楚自己的計畫是什麼；我相信一般年輕作者也同樣是這樣摸索著前進的。
> I know how my own books, especially the earlier ones, got cut about, rearranged, altered in scheme, and cobbled to hide alteration, so that I never fairly knew what my scheme was till the book was three-quarters done, and I credit young writers generally with a like tentativeness.

〔U1328 para〕
我相信我現在已經提出了足夠的理由，讓人認為卷九至卷十二，也就是奧德修斯繞行西西里的航程，是《奧德賽》最早寫成的部分。我之所以更堅信這個看法，是因為我發現奧德修斯用喀耳刻教他的一種結把箱子繫牢（卷八 448 行）──彷彿作者對喀耳刻已經瞭如指掌，雖然聽眾當然還不可能知道她。此外，卷二 19 行顯示作者知道卷九的內容，這一段在我的縮譯裡沒有出現。我們在這裡得知安提福斯是怎樣被波呂斐摩斯吃掉的；卷一 68 行講到奧德修斯弄瞎了庫克羅普斯，也是以卷九為前提的。
> I have now, I believe, shown sufficient cause for thinking that Books ix.-xii., *i.e*., the voyage of Ulysses round Sicily, were the part of the *Odyssey* that was written first. I am further confirmed in this opinion by finding Ulysses fasten his box with a knot that Circe had taught him (viii. 448)—as though the writer knew all about Circe, though the audience, of course, could not yet do so. A knowledge of Book ix., moreover, is shown in Book ii. 19, a passage which does not appear in my abridgement. Here we learn how Antiphus had been eaten by Polyphemus; Book ix. is also presupposed in i. 68, which tells of the blinding of the Cyclops by Ulysses.

〔U1329 para〕
我們也可以有把握地說，卷五至卷八寫在卷一至卷四以及卷十三至卷二十四（大致而言）之前；但卷五至卷八經歷過哪些變遷，它們是否取材自更早的少女時代習作──我雖然拿不出一絲一毫的證據，卻仍傾向這樣認為──這些問題，就連試圖去斷定，都只是浪費時間。
> We may also confidently say that Books v.—viii. were written before i.-iv. and xiii.-xxiv. (roughly), but what the vicissitudes of Books v.-viii. were, and whether or no they drew upon earlier girlish sketches—as without one shred of evidence in support of my opinion I nevertheless incline to think—these are points which it would be a waste of time to even attempt to determine.

〔U1330 para〕
作者最得心應手的，就是卷五至卷八，尤其是其中後三卷。繆爾上校談到斯刻里亞時說的話，很少有人會不同意：──
> It is in Books v.-viii., and especially in the three last of these books, that the writer is most in her element. Few will differ from Col. Mure, who says of Scheria:—

〔U1331 para〕
從詩人賦予當地居民的那些鮮明特徵，以及他描繪他們性格時所展現的精確與力道看來，幾乎無可懷疑，這段插曲是要諷刺他所熟悉的某個真實民族的習性。
> There can be little doubt from the distinctive peculiarities with which the poet has invested its inhabitants, and the precision and force displayed in his portrait of their character, that the episode was intended as a satire on the habits of some real people with whom he was familiar.

〔U1332 para〕
（《古希臘語言文學批判史》，第一卷，第 404 頁）。
> (*Language and Literature of Ancient Greece*, Vol. I., p. 404).

〔U1333 para〕
繆爾上校在同一頁談到費埃克斯插曲顯然出於詼諧的構思時，說道：──
> Speaking on the same page of the obviously humorous spirit in which the Phæacian episode is conceived, Col. Mure says:—-

〔U1334 quote〕
這段插曲或許是詩人結合刻畫人物的才能與諷刺幽默的才能最出色的範例。他的作品中，沒有哪一部分比這段更是充分評價他的天才所不可或缺的；然而或許也沒有哪一部分像這段一樣，這麼少被人理解。只要看看最受推崇的注疏的大意，或許更要看看最通行譯本的本文就知道了：通篇調劑著整段奇遇的那種輕快而帶譏諷的描寫與對話語調，在那裡竟被換成了《伊里亞德》最莊重場面那種悲劇式的肅穆。
> This episode is, perhaps, the most brilliant specimen of the poet's combined talent for the delineation of character and for satirical humour. While there is no portion of his works a right understanding of which is so indispensable to a full estimate of his genius, there is none, perhaps, which has been so little understood. Appeal may be made to the tenor of the most esteemed commentaries, still more, perhaps, to the text of the most popular translations, where the gay sarcastic tone of description and dialogue which seasons the whole adventure, is replaced by the tragic solemnity of the gravest scones of the *Iliad*.

〔U1335 para〕
人只會找到自己帶去的東西。傑出的荷馬學者在《奧德賽》比較詼諧的部分找到那麼多嚴肅，會不會正是因為他們自己把嚴肅帶了進去？在嚴肅的人眼裡，萬事都是嚴肅的。我從戈蘭茨先生《坦普爾版莎士比亞》書末的註釋得知，柯立芝看不出《哈姆雷特》裡穿插的那些伶人台詞有什麼戲仿的意味。他說：──
> People find what they bring. Is it possible that eminent Homeric scholars have found so much seriousness in the more humorous parts of the *Odyssey* because they brought it there? To the serious all things are serious. Coleridge, so I learn from the notes at the end of Mr. Gollancz's *Temple Shakespeare*, saw no burlesque in the speeches of the players which are introduced into *Hamlet*. He says:—

〔U1336 quote〕
以為這裡有意戲仿，這種想法根本不值一駁；這些詩句作為史詩敘事，堪稱絕妙。
> The fancy that a burlesque was intended sinks below criticism; the lines, as epic narrative, are superb.

〔U1337 para〕
既然戈蘭茨先生沒有註明出處，我也只好不註。戈蘭茨先生接著說，柯立芝假如讀過《蒂朵與埃涅阿斯》第二幕第一場──那是馬羅未完成的一齣戲──他就會改變看法；但我不相信他會改。
> As Mr. Gollancz has given no reference, so neither can I. Mr. Gollancz continues that if Coleridge had read Act II. Scene i. of *Dido* and *Æneas*—a play left unfinished by Marlowe—he would have changed his mind, but I do not believe he would.

〔U1338 para〕
話雖如此，我認為作者是一半說笑、一半認真，有時候連她自己恐怕也很難說清楚，究竟是說笑的成分多，還是認真的成分多。同樣地，知道清唱劇《納西瑟斯》（本書書末登有它的廣告）的人會承認：有些人完全明白，世上沒有任何音樂比得上韓德爾的音樂那樣偉大，卻仍然要嘗試以韓德爾的風格作曲；等他們寫完了，幾乎說不清自己是說笑多些還是認真多些，雖然在寫的時候，他們完全相信自己不過是盡己所能，寫出韓德爾若晚生一百年左右、為這樣的歌詞譜曲時會寫的那種音樂。
> At the same time I take it that the writer was one half laughing and the other half serious, and would sometimes have been hard put to it to know whether she was more in the one vein than in the other. So those who know the cantata *Narcissus*, advertised at the end of this volume, will admit that there are people who are fully aware that there is no music in this world so great as Handel's, but who will still try to write music in the style of Handel, and when they have done it, hardly know whether they have been more in jest or earnest, though while doing it they fully believed that they were only writing, so far as in them lay, the kind of music which Handel would have written for such words had he lived a hundred years or so later than he did.

〔U1339 para〕
我們可以注意到──雖然無法據此推斷出這首詩各部分寫成的年代──在《奧德賽》的前四卷裡，季節似乎是夏天而非冬天。在其他各卷裡（當然不算奧德修斯講述自己經歷的那幾卷），季節無疑是冬天，或者是初春時節。同樣值得注意的是，雪在《伊里亞德》裡一再出現，荷馬顯然深深感受到雪的美，但在《奧德賽》裡卻沒有出現，幾乎連提都沒提到。我或許應該告訴某些讀者：特洛阿斯的冬天又長又嚴酷，而西西里西岸幾乎從不下雪，冬天甚至比阿爾及爾還要溫和。
> We may note, without, however, being able to deduce anything from it as regards the dates at which the various parts of the poem were composed, that in the first four Books of the *Odyssey* the season appears to be summer rather than winter. In all the other Books (of course excluding those in which Ulysses tells his story) the season is unquestionably winter, or very early spring. It is noticeable also that snow, which appears so repeatedly in the *Iliad*, and of which Homer evidently felt the beauty very strongly, does not appear, and is hardly even mentioned, in the *Odyssey*. I should perhaps tell some readers that winter is long and severe in the Troad, while on the West coast of Sicily snow is almost unknown, and the winter is even milder than that of Algiers.

〔U1340 para〕
我或許也不該略過這個事實：琥珀在《伊里亞德》裡從未提及，在《奧德賽》裡卻出現了三次。[註4]這也許純屬偶然；不過西西里是個出產琥珀的地方，事實上至今仍是；恩納古城，也就是今天的卡斯特羅焦萬尼，那裡的博物館藏有大批西西里琥珀；我還從可靠的來源聽說──只是我沒有查證告訴我的人所言是否屬實──南肯辛頓博物館也能見到一些精美的標本。《大英百科全書》談到西西里琥珀時說：──
> I ought also perhaps hardly to pass over the fact that amber, which is never mentioned in the *Iliad*, appears three times in the *Odyssey*.[註4] This may be mere accident, nevertheless Sicily was an amber-producing country, and indeed still is so; a large collection of Sicilian amber exists in the museum of Castrogiovanni, the ancient Enna, and I have been assured on good authority, but have not verified my informant's statement, that some fine specimens may be seen in the South Kensington Museum. Speaking of Sicilian amber the *Encyclopedia Britannica* says:—

〔U1341 quote〕
最美的標本，或許要算在卡塔尼亞發現的那些。它們常常閃現一種美麗的紫色光澤，這是其他產地的琥珀所看不到的。
> The most beautiful specimens are, perhaps, those which are found at Catania. They often possess a beautiful play of purple not to be observed in the product of other places.

〔U1342 para〕
我弄不清楚前四卷是寫在後十二卷之前還是之後；大概是先寫的，但反面也有一些理由可說。我不打算去解決這個問題，只想再補充一點：只要我們記得，《奧德賽》的兩大部分──費埃克斯插曲連同奧德修斯的歸鄉，以及潘妮洛碧與求婚者的故事──都顯示出明白無誤的跡象，表明它們寫於同一地點，出自女性之手，而且出自一位顯然還非常年輕的女性之手，又記得在這兩部分之間，找不出一絲一毫格律、風格或語言習慣上的差異，那麼我們應當得出的唯一結論就是：這首詩從第一頁到最後一頁，都出自同一位女性之手。我想，在沒有任何相反證據的情況下──因為確實沒有任何稱得上證據的證據──我們也可以斷定，我們手上這首詩，實際上就是它在女作者手中定稿時的樣子。
> I cannot make out whether the first four Books were written before the last twelve or after; probably they were written first, but there is something to be said also on the other side. I will not attempt to settle this point, and will only add that when we hear in mind how both the two main divisions of the *Odyssey*—the Phæacian episode with the Return of Ulysses, and the story of Penelope and the suitors, show unmistakeable signs of having been written at one place, by woman, by woman who is evidently still very young, and that not a trace of difference in versification, style, or idiom can be found between the two divisions, the only conclusion we should come to is that the poem was written by one and the same woman from the first page to the last. I think we may also conclude in the absence of all evidence to the contrary—for assuredly none exists that deserves the name of evidence—that we have the poem to all intents and purposes in the shape which it had assumed in the hands of the authoress.

〔U1343 footnote〕
《荷馬導論》，麥克米倫出版，1888 年，第 172 頁。
> *Introduction to Homer*, Macmillan, 1888, p. 172.

這三段在我的縮譯裡都找不到。
> None of these three passages will be found in my abridgement.

〔U1347 heading〕
第十六章
> CHAPTER XVI.

〔U1348 heading〕
結論
> CONCLUSION.

〔U1349 para〕
在我結束這個題目之前，或許應該回答一個讀者大概早就在心裡問過的問題。我是說：前面各章所提出的種種考量既然這麼明顯，假如其中真有幾分道理，怎麼可能讓這麼多能幹的學者在這麼多個世紀裡都視而不見？因為這些考量全都擺在表面上；它們不過就像到約旦河裡洗一洗就得潔淨那麼簡單；它們什麼都不要求，只要求一個人像讀其他任何一本書那樣讀《奧德賽》，留意斯刻里亞和伊薩卡場景裡描寫的地形地貌，然後到希臘以西、地中海的某處西岸去找它們。
> Before I quit my subject, I should perhaps answer a question which the reader has probably long since asked himself. I mean, how it is conceivable that considerations so obvious as those urged in the foregoing Chapters should have been overlooked by so many capable students for so many hundreds of years, if there were any truth in them. For they lie all of them upon the surface; they are a mere washing in the Jordan and being clean; they require nothing but that a person should read the *Odyssey* as he would any other book, noting the physical characters described in the Scherian and Ithacan scenes, and looking for them on some West coast of the Mediterranean to the West of Greece.

〔U1350 para〕
答案是：我所提出的這些考量之所以一直被忽略，是因為基於非常明顯的理由，從來沒有人想到要去找它們。不只一位傑出學者曾經直接或間接地對我說：「那麼，你以為除了你，就沒有人讀過《奧德賽》嗎？」我當然沒有這樣以為；我知道，真相一旦失落（腓尼基人在德列帕農站穩腳跟之後，真相很快就會失落），要再被發現，只有等到人們確信《奧德賽》並非《伊里亞德》的作者所寫的時候，才有可能。這個想法普遍為人接受，至今還不到一百年，[註1]甚至可能更短；可是在人們牢牢抓住這個想法之前，誰也不大可能懷疑《奧德賽》會出自西西里，更別說懷疑它出自女性之手了；因為《伊里亞德》裡沒有一行詩暗示過西西里的存在，或者讓讀者懷疑作者是個女人，反倒有不計其數的段落，似乎完全排除了作者是男人──而且是個非常強悍的男人──以外的任何看法。上個世紀的施托爾貝格和本世紀的繆爾上校，造訪特拉帕尼的時候，鑰匙其實已經插進鎖孔了；兩人都滿心確信，庫克羅普斯那段情節以及獵山羊的事，應該定位在埃律克斯山與法維尼亞納島──但他們沒有轉動鑰匙。弗里曼教授、施里曼與雷亞德爵士，都曾以研究者或發掘者的身分造訪特拉帕尼及其近郊，卻都沒有看出那裡埋著一份輝煌的寶藏，堪與尼尼微、邁錫尼和希沙利克的寶藏相比，而且挖掘它不用鎬也不用鏟，不必花錢，也不費任何力氣──為什麼？因為他們仍然受制於長久以來把《伊里亞德》和《奧德賽》看作同一人作品的觀念。他們既然知道《伊里亞德》幾乎不可能寫於小亞細亞西岸北半部以外的地方，自然絕不會想到要到特拉帕尼這麼遙遠的地方去找《奧德賽》。他們大概把它看作某位史前希羅多德的作品，從一個場景走到另一個場景，在任何一地都不肯多停留一刻；而不是像我相信他們本該做到的那樣，確信它出自一位大概從未離家超過寥寥幾英里的女子之手。何況英國海軍部海圖是相當晚近才有的東西，我想若沒有它們的幫助，誰也不大可能把《奧德賽》追到它的老窩。
> The answer is that the considerations which I have urged have been overlooked because, for very obvious reasons, it never occurred to any one to look for them. "Do you suppose, then," more than one eminent scholar has said to me directly or indirectly, "that no one has ever read the *Odyssey* except yourself?" I suppose nothing of the kind, and know that it was only possible for the truth when once lost (as it soon would be on the establishment of the Phœnicians at Drepanum) to be rediscovered, when people had become convinced that the *Odyssey* was not written by the writer of the *Iliad*. This idea has not yet been generally accepted for more than a hundred years,[註1] if so long, but until it was seized and held firmly, no one was likely to suspect that the *Odyssey* could have come from Sicily, much less that it could have been written by a woman, for there is not one line in the *Iliad* which even hints at the existence of Sicily, or makes the reader suspect the author to have been a woman, while there are any number of passages which seem absolutely prohibitive of any other opinion than that the writer was a man, and a very strong one. Stolberg in the last century, and Colonel Mure in this, had the key in the lock when they visited Trapani, each of them with the full conviction that the Cyclops incident, and the hunting the goats, should be placed on Mt. Eryx and the island of Favognana—but they did not turn it. Professor Freeman, Schliemann, and Sir H. Layard, all of them visited Trapani and its immediate neighbourhood either as students or excavators, and failed to see that there was as splendid a prize to be unburied there without pick and shovel, outlay, or trouble of any kind, as those of Nineveh, Mycene, and Hissarlik—and why? Because they were still hampered by the long association of the *Iliad* and *Odyssey* as the work of the same person. Knowing that the *Iliad* could hardly have been written elsewhere than in the Northern half of the West coast of Asia Minor, it would never occur to them to look for the *Odyssey* in a spot so remote as Trapani. They probably held it to be the work of some prehistoric Herodotus, who would go on from scene to scene without staying longer than he could help in any one place, instead of feeling sure, as I believe they should have done, that it was the work of one who was little likely to have travelled more than a very few miles from her own home. Moreover, Admiralty charts are things of comparatively recent date, and I do not think any one would have been likely to have run the *Odyssey* to ground without their help.

〔U1351 para〕
不過，不管情形如何，我毫不懷疑，把《奧德賽》歸於荷馬的習慣，正是大家看不出與它相關的那些明顯事實的主要原因。說真的，我們那些研究《伊里亞德》和《奧德賽》的傑出學者，也該停止把《奧德賽》收進他們的「荷馬」作品「導論」裡，藉此誤導自己、也誤導別人了。直到最近幾年為止，這樣做還說得過去；事實上，若不這樣做，反倒顯得迂腐；可是一百年前，不這樣做會顯得迂腐；到了今天，還這樣做就是馬虎、不夠學術了。
> But however this may be, I do not doubt that the habit of ascribing the *Odyssey* to Homer has been the main reason of the failure to see the obvious in connection with it. Surely it is time our eminent Iliadic and Odyssean scholars left off misleading themselves and other people by including the *Odyssey* in their "Introductions" to the work of "Homer." It was permissible to do this till within recent years; anything else, indeed, would have been pedantic, but what would have been pedantic a hundred years ago, is slovenly and unscholarly now.

〔U1352 para〕
從評注她的人轉到女作者本人身上，我不禁想知道：她要是知道自己這麼久以來一直被誤認為男人──而且那個男人還是荷馬──究竟會比較高興，還是比較生氣。這會給她一個絕佳的機會去嘲笑男人的遲鈍。不過，她固然無疑會生氣，同時恐怕也免不了覺得受寵若驚；她或許還會這樣自我安慰：像她這樣偉大的詩人，注定要為偉大付出被誤解的代價。
> Turning from her commentators to the authoress herself, I am tempted to wonder whether she would be more pleased or angry could she know that she had been so long mistaken for a man—and that man Homer. It would afford her an excellent opportunity for laughing at the dullness of man. Angry, however, as she would no doubt be, she could hardly at the same time help being flattered, and would perhaps console herself by reflecting that poets as great as she was are bound to pay the penalty of greatness in being misunderstood.

〔U1353 para〕
賀拉斯告訴我們，詩人要是平庸，神、人和出版商都不會容許；可是不管容不容許，總有好些平庸的詩人過得還算不錯。說實在的，照我看來，神、人，尤其是出版商，除了平庸之外，什麼都不會容許詩人有；如果有一位真正的詩人因為某種罕見的意外混進了其他詩人之中，那是因為神和出版社的審稿人沒能及早識破他，等發現時已經來不及阻止了。賀拉斯一定完全明白自己是在胡說八道。
> Horace tells us that mediocrity in a poet is forbidden alike by gods, men, and publishers, but, whether forbidden or no, there are a good many mediocre poets who are doing fairly well. So far as I can see, indeed, gods, men, and more particularly publishers, will tolerate nothing in a poet except mediocrity, and if a true poet by some rare accident slips in among the others, it is because gods and publisher's readers did not find him out until it was too late to stop him. Horace must have known perfectly well that he was talking nonsense.

〔U1354 para〕
說到底，事情就是這樣也好；因為平庸的詩人雖然可能苟延殘喘許多年，最後終究會死去，或者至少會變成一個不折不扣的文學界斯楚德布魯格，不再給老實人添任何麻煩；而真正的詩人卻會佔據我們，不管我們願不願意，都在我們身上活下去；這種人的數量若不嚴加控制，就會卡住世界的輪子。散文或韻文的一流詩人有半打，就已是世界所能安然承受的極限了；二十個莎士比亞、二十個荷馬、二十個瑙西卡，會使文學無以為繼；然而我們可以確信，假如天才能立刻被認出來，並且由那些唯一懂得如何培育天才的人加以培育，那麼每個國家在每個世紀都能產生兩三位一流作家。天才是一種冒犯；正如其他一切冒犯，它必然要來，但那個讓它降臨的男人或女人有禍了，因為他或她必須穿過斯庫拉與卡律布狄斯：一邊是被撕成碎片，另一邊是遭人誤解到這種地步，以致靠著這番曲解保住性命、僥倖溜過去，比死還要叫人難堪。
> And after all it is well that things are as they are; for the mediocre poet, though he may hang about for many years, does in the end die, or at any rate become such a mere literary Struldbrug as to give plain people no trouble, whereas the true poet will possess himself of us, and live on in us whether we will or no, and unless the numbers of such people were severely kept in check they would clog the wheels of the world. Half a dozen first-class poets in prose or verse are as many as the world can carry in any comfort; twenty Shakspeares, twenty Homers, twenty Nausicaas would make literature impossible, yet we may be sure that every country in every century could yield two or three first-class writers, if genius were to be known at once and fostered by those who alone know how to foster it. Genius is an offence; like all other offences it must needs come, but woe to that man or woman through whom it comes, for he or she must pass through the Scylla and Charybdis of being either torn in pieces on the one hand, or so misunderstood on the other as to make the slipping through with life in virtue of such misrepresentation more mortifying than death itself.

〔U1355 para〕
不管我們怎麼做，都無可奈何。死去的心智就像死去的身體一樣，若要讓活的心智得到公平的機會，就必須在適當的間隔之後，從我們眼前埋葬掉；我們那些大教育機構若能比現在多一點火葬場的味道、少一點地下墓穴的味道，或許也不是壞事。我們關於心智衛生的觀念不完善得可悲；活人若不更加警惕，不讓死去的心智在體內留著不消化，不久之後必定會發作一場心智上的痛風，就算不致命，也會痛得死去活來。既然天才一旦被我們吸收，要排除他們就有如此難以克服的困難；又既然不管我們怎麼做，都無法從一大群資質中等的好孩子當中，認出可能誕生其間的那一個天才，就像希律無法從伯利恆的嬰孩中認出猶太人的王一樣，那麼我們別無他法，只能照希律的做法大致做一遍：對所有年輕人下毒手，直到把他們每一個都壓成平庸之輩，平庸到可以放心他們遲早會自行退場為止。為了這個目的，我們設立了學校和經院學者；而且也很難想像，還有什麼辦法能更有效地培養那種自滿──它大大有助於我們在世上混下去──同時又壓制任何過剩的原創力或思想獨立；這些東西在擁有者生前可能對他不利，在他死後又會成為後人的負擔。
> Do what we may we cannot help it. Dead mind like dead body must, after a decent interval, be buried out of our sight if living mind is to have fair play, and it might perhaps not be a bad thing if our great educational establishments had more of the crematorium and less of the catacomb about them than they have at present. Our notions of intellectual sanitation are deplorably imperfect, and unless the living become more jealous of letting dead mind remain unconsumed in their system, a fit of intellectual gout must ere long supervene, which, if not fatal, will still be excruciatingly painful. Since, therefore, there are such insuperable difficulties in the way of eliminating geniuses when we have once absorbed them, and since also, do what we may, we can no more detect the one genius who may be born among a multitude of good average children, than Herod could detect the King of the Jews among the babes of Bethlehem, we have no course but to do much as Herod did, and lay violent hands upon all young people till we have reduced every single one of them to such mediocrity as may be trusted to take itself off sooner or later. To this end we have established schools and schoolmen; nor is it easy to see how we could more effectually foster that self-sufficiency which does so much towards helping us through the world, and yet repress any exuberance of originality or independence of thought which may be prejudicial to its possessor during his own life, and burdensome to posterity when he is dead and gone.

〔U1356 para〕
然而，我們現行的制度儘管明顯明智而必要，我們還是對它怨聲載道。我們想要藝術、文學和音樂上有數不清的一流天才，同時又要自己有充裕的迴旋餘地。我們的孩子也是如此：他們顯露再多的天才跡象都不嫌多，可是同時，他們要是在世上混不出名堂、賺不了錢──而天才幾乎從來都賺不了錢──我們就要怪他們。我們就像《奧德賽》的女作者一樣，總想兩頭兼得；我們希望別人被遺忘，自己卻不被遺忘；當我們擺脫了這具凡軀的糾纏之後，還巴望在後世子孫的美好追念裡，再套上另一具──糾纏少些、凡俗也少些；但這種願望即使普遍到可以稱為天性，我們當中最優秀、最清醒的人也會與之抗爭，而不會助長它；這樣的人會盡其所能、愉快地把工作做好，等到他們的時代過去了，就盡量不聲不響地讓位給別人。
> Obviously wise, however, and necessary as our present system is, we nevertheless grumble at it. We would have any number of first-class geniuses in art literature and music, and yet have plenty of elbow room for ourselves. Our children too; they cannot show too many signs of genius, but at the same time we blame them if they do not get on in the world and make money as genius next to never does. Like the authoress of the *Odyssey* we are always wanting to have things both ways; we would have others be forgotten, and yet not be forgotten ourselves; when we have shuffled off this mortal coil, we would fain shuffle on another that shall be at once less coil and less mortal, in the good thoughts of coming generations, but if this desire is so universal as to be called natural, it is one which the best and sanest of us will fight against rather than encourage; such people will do their work as well and cheerfully as they can, and make room for others with as little fuss as possible when they have had their day.

〔U1357 para〕
然而，假如有人憤恨於自然的常規，開始顧影自憐，咒罵自己的命運，怨自己生來不屬於那些即使在今世也能進入永生的人之列，那就讓他這樣想來安慰自己吧：在他死去很久以前，誰也無法確知他究竟是活著還是沒有；而且即使他最後證明是個不朽之人，他也逃不掉那種待遇──他越是不朽，就越肯定要受到那種待遇──更別提他的作品將會給年輕人帶來的無窮苦難了。
> If, however, any man resents the common course of nature and sets himself to looking upon himself and cursing his fate that he was not born to be of the number of them that enter into life eternal even in this world, let him console himself by reflecting that until he is long dead, there is no certain knowing whether he is in life or no, and also that though he prove to be an immortal after all, he cannot escape the treatment which he is the more sure to meet with according as he is the more immortal—let alone the untold misery which his works will inflict upon young people.

〔U1358 para〕
假如曾經有哪一位偉大的經典作家，會因為知道後世將如何對待她而不敢動筆，那麼《奧德賽》的作者就該如此，因為從來沒有一首這麼容易理解的詩，被誤解得這麼徹底。假如她真像我願意想像的那樣美麗，那麼可曾有哪位睡美人，被藏在一道比這經院學問更難穿越的荊棘籬笆後面？又怎麼可能不是這樣呢？《奧德賽》和《伊里亞德》一樣，當了將近三千年的教科書，遲鈍對天才還能施加什麼比這更殘酷的報復？過去兩千五百年的博學，對《伊里亞德》和《奧德賽》做了什麼？不過是在小處校訂字句，在大處遮蔽精神罷了。
> If ever a great classic could have been deterred from writing by a knowledge of how posterity would treat her, the writer of the *Odyssey* should have been so, for never has poem more easy to understand failed more completely of being understood. If she was as lovely as I should like to think her, was ever sleeping beauty hidden behind a more impenetrable hedge of scholasticism? How could it be otherwise? The *Odyssey*, like the *Iliad*, has been a school book for nearly 3,000 years, and what more cruel revenge could dullness take on genius? What has the erudition of the last 2,500 years done for the *Iliad* and the *Odyssey* but to emend the letter in small things and to obscure the spirit in great ones?

〔U1359 para〕
誠然，如我在開頭那一章所說，在基督誕生前一兩個世紀，曾有一群學者不肯把《伊里亞德》和《奧德賽》看作同一人的作品；但博學對他們嗤之以鼻，把他們掐滅得如此徹底，以致大約兩千年來，他們都被認為已經被徹底駁倒了。對經院批評的價值，還有比這更辛辣的諷刺嗎？看來雅典娜彷彿在這兩首詩上，罩了她曾經罩在奧德修斯身上的那層黑暗，好讓它們一代又一代在傑出的荷馬學者之間進進出出，卻沒有一個人看見。
> There was indeed, as I said in my opening Chapter, a band of scholars a century or two before the birth of Christ who refused to see the *Iliad* and *Odyssey* as the work of the same person, but erudition snubbed them and snuffed them out so effectually that for some 2,000 years they were held to have been finally refuted. Can there be any more scathing satire on the value of scholastic criticism? It seems as though Minerva had shed the same darkness over both the poems that she shed over Ulysses, that they might go in and out among eminent Homeric scholars from generation to generation, and none should see them.

〔U1360 para〕
世界對它最偉大的男男女女的確所知甚少，也一直因為缺乏洞察力而飽受責難；但凡事總有兩面，我們也應該記得，世上最偉大的男男女女，通常對世界比較因循俗套的那些面向也所知甚少。他們老是違背我們對偉大人物的一切期待，而且從來不告訴我們他們是什麼人；他們甚至不認為自己偉大；如果他們這樣認為，我們大可確定他們弄錯了；那麼，在我們有充分的時間把他們好好想一想之前，又怎麼能指望我們正確地評價他們呢？
> The world does indeed know little of its greatest men and women, and bitterly has it been reproached for its want of penetration, but there are always two sides, and it should be remembered that its greatest men and women commonly know very little of the world in its more conventional aspects. They are continually flying in the face of all that we expect of greatness, and they never tell us what they are; they do not even think that they are great; if they do we may be sure that they are mistaken; how then can we be expected to appreciate people correctly till we have had plenty of time to think them over?

〔U1361 para〕
而等我們把他們想過了，我們的批評準則，和我們最後得出的評斷又有多少關係呢？看看《奧德賽》吧。這首詩開篇之前，男女主角早已結婚多年；從第一頁到最後一頁，沒有一對相愛的年輕男女，事實上全詩根本沒有什麼談情說愛的成分，因為雖然求婚者照理說都瘋狂愛著潘妮洛碧，他們卻從來沒有說過或做過什麼，讓人信服他們真的如此。我們接受這個事實，就像接受奧德修斯的睿智一樣，是因為有人這麼告訴我們，而不是因為我們看到了。這首詩表面上的主要看點，是一位禿頂的中年紳士──他頭上僅剩的一點頭髮還是紅的──向一群年輕人報仇；這些年輕人一面追求他那位被認為已成寡婦的妻子，一面把他吃得傾家蕩產。
> And when we have thought them over, how little have our canons of criticism to do with the verdict which we in the end arrive at. Look at the *Odyssey*. Here is a poem in which the hero and heroine have been already married many years before it opens; from the first page to the last there is no young couple in love with one another, there is in fact nothing amatory in the poem, for though the suitors are supposed to be madly in love with Penelope, they never say or do anything that carries conviction as to their being so. We accept the fact, as we do the sagacity of Ulysses, because we are told it, not because we see it. The interest of the poem ostensibly turns mainly on the revenge taken by a bald middle-aged gentleman, whose little remaining hair is red, on a number of young men who have been eating him out of house and home, while courting his supposed widow.

〔U1362 para〕
不僅如此，這個先天就有缺陷的題材，處理起來在前後一致與合情合理方面又如此草率，對眾所周知的細節如此無知，對一般準則又如此漠視，簡直很難有誰能超越；然而，藝術的反諷正在這裡：說只有一首詩能毫無疑問地凌駕其上，並不為過。如果說《奧德賽》比任何其他藝術真理都更強調了某一條，那就是：文學上有生命、能長存的作品（藝術與音樂亦然），只有兩種人做得出來：一種人超越於有意識地參照任何規則或準則之上，另一種人則根本還達不到那個層次──而且，儘管有莎士比亞、韓德爾和林布蘭的例子，我還是要說，大體而言，在下比在上更為有福。因為說到底，讓我們滿懷感激與愛慕、拜倒在一部作品腳下的，並不是我們在其中所讀、所見或所聞的那些外在可見的記號；真正打動我們的，是與我們所感念的那位男子或女子仍然活著的心靈相交，並且確信那個心靈正是我們希望自己的心靈所成為的樣子。其餘一切，不過是衣裳和文法而已。
> Moreover, this subject, so initially faulty, is treated with a carelessness in respect of consistency and plausibility, an ignorance of commonly known details, and a disregard of ordinary canons which it would not be easy to surpass, and yet, such is the irony of art that it is not too much to say that there is only one poem which can be decisively placed above it. If the *Odyssey* enforces one artistic truth more than another, it is that living permanent work in literature (and the same holds good for art and music) can only be done by those who are either above, or below, conscious reference to any rules or canons whatsoever—and in spite of Shakespeare, Handel, and Rembrandt, I should say that on the whole it is more blessed to be below than above. For after all it is not the outward and visible signs of what we read, see, or hear, in any work, that bring us to its feet in prostration of gratitude and affection; what really stirs us is the communion with the still living mind of the man or woman to whom we owe it, and the conviction that that mind is as we would have our own to be. All else is mere clothes and grammar.

〔U1363 para〕
說到《奧德賽》作者的心靈，她的作品裡最令我深受感動的，莫過於我在全詩中處處感受到的那股憂鬱的暗流。我不是說作者總是、甚至通常是不快樂的；她常常──至少讓我們這樣希望吧──快樂到了極點；然而她的作品裡始終有一種感覺，彷彿這個世界儘管充滿歡樂，卻還是脫了節──一種說不出口、難以名狀的情緒，一半是哀傷，一半是受挫的憤怒，即使一時隱沒不見，不久又會重新浮現。如果說《奧德賽》不是沒有笑聲寫成的，那麼它也不是沒有眼淚寫成的。如今我既已知道作者是個女人，便為自己感到羞愧：我本該讓瀰漫全詩那種既柔弱又剛強、細膩入微的感受，引導我得出結論，結果引導我的卻是那些實際上引導了我的考量。
> As regards the mind of the writer of the *Odyssey* there is nothing in her work which impresses me more profoundly than the undercurrent of melancholy which I feel throughout it. I do not mean that the writer was always, or indeed generally, unhappy; she was often, at any rate let us hope so, supremely happy; nevertheless there is throughout her work a sense as though the world for all its joyousness was nevertheless out of joint—an inarticulate indefinable half pathos, half baffled fury, which even when lost sight of for a time soon re-asserts itself. If the *Odyssey* was not written without laughter, so neither was it without tears. Now that I know the writer to have been a woman, I am ashamed of myself for not having been guided to my conclusion by the exquisitely subtle sense of weakness as well as of strength that pervades the poem, rather than by the considerations that actually guided me.

〔U1364 para〕
我所見過唯一稱得上論證、用來說明《奧德賽》必定出自男人之手的，就是堅稱沒有女人寫得出奧德修斯殺死求婚者的那一場。我看不出這一點；在我看來，倒不如說沒有男人能夠如此毫無愧疚地罔顧情理；況且女人在紙上殺死一個男人，也和男人一樣在行；而除了奧德修斯饒了費彌俄斯與墨東性命那段可愛的插曲之外，我承認，那一場在我看來是全詩最機械、最不令人滿意的部分。人們之所以不能普遍相信《奧德賽》出自女性之手，真正的障礙並不在詩中能找到的任何東西，而是如我已經說過的，在於長久以來流行的一種看法，認為它和《伊里亞德》出自同一人之手。這種看法年代久遠、德高望重，即使我們終於拋棄了它，它也不容我們跨出一步，最多只能把《奧德賽》歸給另一個男人──我們無法一下子跳到它根本不是男人所寫的看法。某種牢不可破的經院習氣，使我們看不見本來一眼就能看見的東西──只要我們肯慢慢讀這首詩，而且不去考慮批評家對它說過的任何話。
> The only approach to argument which I have seen brought forward to show that the *Odyssey* must have been written by a man, consists in maintaining that no woman could have written the scene in which Ulysses kills the suitors. I cannot see this; to me it seems rather that no man could have brought himself to disregard probability with so little compunction; moreover a woman can kill a man on paper as well as a man can, and with the exception of the delightful episode in which Ulysses spares the lives of Phemius and Medon, the scene, I confess, appears to me to be the most mechanical and least satisfactory in the whole poem. The real obstacle to a general belief that the *Odyssey* was written by a woman is not anything that can be found in the poem, but lies, as I have already said, in the long prevalence of an opinion that it was written by the same person as the *Iliad* was. The age and respectability of this opinion, even though we have at length discarded it, will not allow us to go beyond ascribing the *Odyssey* to another man—we cannot jump all at once to the view that it was not by a man at all. A certain invincible scholasticism prevents us from being able to see what we should see at once if we would only read the poem slowly and without considering anything that critics have said concerning it.

〔U1365 para〕
然而，這並不是一件容易做到的事。我很清楚，假如我沒有度過大約三十五年叛逆的歲月，那段期間對《奧德賽》連想都沒想過，我是絕不可能做到的。這首詩是如此莊嚴：它受到這麼多世代的崇敬而成為聖物；它就像我的卷首插圖，那樣神祕，那樣不完美，卻又那樣神妙地超越一切完美；它又那麼長久地與那部至高無上的史詩聯繫在一起──因為如果說《奧德賽》是文學界的羅莎峰，那麼我想，《伊里亞德》必定永遠是白朗峰；面對一部具有如此難以言喻之聲望的作品，誰能輕率地對它活體解剖，彷彿它只是一本沒人留意的 *parvenu*（暴發戶）書，在舊書攤上花幾便士就能撿到？輕率，不行；但若是要藉此恢復它天然的健康與美麗，那就毫不留情，可以。
> This, however, is not an easy thing to do. I know very well that I should never have succeeded in doing it if I had not passed some five-and-thirty rebellious years during which I never gave the *Odyssey* so much as a thought. The poem is so august: it is hallowed by the veneration of so many ages; it is like my frontispiece, so mysterious, so imperfect, and yet so divinely beyond all perfection; it has been so long associated with the epic poem which stands supreme—for if the *Odyssey* be the Monte Rosa of literature, the *Iliad* must, I suppose, for ever remain as the Mont Blanc; who can lightly vivisect a work of such ineffable prestige as though it were an overlooked *parvenu* book picked up for a few pence at a second hand book stall? Lightly, no, but inexorably, yes, if its natural health and beauty are to be restored by doing so.

〔U1366 para〕
一兩年前，我們當今最有造詣的一位學者，就在這一點上責備過我。他說我冷酷無情。他說：「我承認，你那麼看重的那些細節，我不大放在心上：我讀這首詩不是為了對它建立理論，而是為了陶醉在它驚人的美之中。」
> One of our most accomplished living scholars chided with me in this sense a year or two ago. He said I was ruthless. "I confess," he said, "I do not give much heed to the details on which you lay so much stress: I read the poem not to theorise about it, but to revel in its amazing beauty."

〔U1367 para〕
要是我以為自己做了什麼損害那份美感的事，我會大為震驚；我相信我對那份美的感受和他不相上下；可是，如果《奧德賽》作為男人的作品就已經使我們著迷，那麼當我們把它看作女人的作品時，它的魅力與奇妙無疑是無限地增加了。而當我們發現作者原來是一位老朋友，並且看到她作品中不小的一部分映照出她自己的周遭環境時，它就更必然讓我們著迷了。
> It would shock me to think that I had done anything to impair the sense of that beauty which I trust I share in even measure with himself, but surely if the *Odyssey* has charmed us as a man's work, its charm and wonder are infinitely increased when we see it as a woman's. Still more must it charm us when we find the writer to be an old friend, and see no inconsiderable part of her work as a reflection of her own surroundings.

〔U1368 para〕
那麼，我們真的有權在冷靜嚴肅的態度下這樣看待她嗎？我已經說明，在已知最早的希臘文學時代，女詩人為數眾多，而且享有很高的聲譽。我已經說明，大家一致公認，《奧德賽》裡家庭與女性的旨趣遠遠壓過男性的旨趣。我已經說明，這首詩全部寫於同一個地方，既然如此──即使沒有其他理由這樣認為──大概也出自同一人之手：我已經說明，作者對女性的名譽極為在意，在意到為了擺脫一個她認為侮辱了女性的故事，任何不可能都嚇不倒她。既然如此，要求讀者相信這首詩並不像本特利所主張的那樣，是男人為女人而寫，而是由一位本身就是女性的人，為男人和女人共同而寫，這樣的要求難道過分嗎？
> Have we, then, a right in sober seriousness so to find her? I have shown that in the earliest known ages of Greek literature poetesses abounded, and gained a high reputation. I have shown that by universal consent the domestic and female interest in the *Odyssey* predominates greatly over the male. I have shown that it was all written in one place, and if so—even were there no further reasons for thinking so—presumably by one hand: I have shown that the writer was extremely jealous for the honour of woman, so much so as to be daunted by no impossibilities when trying to get rid of a story that she held to be an insult to her sex. These things being so, is it too much to ask the reader to believe that the poem was not written, as Bentley held, by a man for women, but for both men and women, by one who was herself a woman?

〔U1369 para〕
現在，在我向讀者告別之際，我想說：如果說我開始寫這部作品時，曾因想到要讓荷馬學者認真看待並加以考慮是多麼無望而感到沉重，那麼現在當我翻閱它的書頁，看到它必定會惹惱許多我寧可設法討好、而不願得罪的人，我更是感到沉重而沮喪。《奧德賽》在哪裡寫成，或者是男人還是女人寫的，對我又有什麼關係？我可以從心底真誠地說，這些問題最後怎麼定案，我並不在乎；但我確實在乎，而且非常在乎，想知道那些考慮過我在本書中所提論點的明理之人，會把它們定在哪一邊。我相信這兩點我都已經充分解決了；但不論結果如何，我知道我在這兩點上的論據都十分有力，足以證明我把它提出來是有道理的。那麼，我就把它留在這裡了。
> And now as I take leave of the reader, I would say that if when I began this work I was oppressed with a sense of the hopelessness of getting Homeric scholars to take it seriously and consider it, I am even more oppressed and dismayed when I turn over its pages and see how certain they are to displease many whom I would far rather conciliate than offend. What can it matter to me where the *Odyssey* was written, or whether it was written by a man or a woman? From the bottom of my heart I can say truly that I do not care about the way in which these points are decided, but I do care, and very greatly, about knowing which way they are decided by sensible people who have considered what I have urged in this book. I believe I have settled both points sufficiently, but come what may I know that my case in respect of them is amply strong enough to justify me in having stated it. And so I leave it.

〔U1370 footnote〕
我看到我的祖父，什魯斯伯里的巴特勒博士，在他 1813 年出版的《古代地理》中接受了這個看法，但我不知道他是從哪裡得來的。
> I see that my grandfather, Dr. Butler, of Shrewsbury, accepts it in his *Antient Geography*, published in 1813, but I do not know where he got it from.
